<!-- head-1: body_md -->
Our first main result is to prove that the asymptotic behaviour of minimizing sequences can be fully described by looking at the capillarity energy on asymptotic cylinders of the obstacle $C$. 
        We do so by generalizing competitors outside $C$ to asymptotic configurations, which include competitors outside the asymptotic cylinders.
        \begin{definition}\label{def: asymptotic configuration}
			Let $C\subset\mathbb R^N$ be a closed convex set.
            We call the couple $(\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{C})$ an \emph{asymptotic configuration} for $C$ if there exists $k\in \mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}$ such that
            \begin{equation}
				\mathbf E=(E_0, \ldots, E_k), \qquad \mathbf C=(C,C_1, \ldots, C_k),
			\end{equation} 
            where $C_1, \ldots, C_k$ are asymptotic cylinders for $C$, $E_0\subset \mathbb R^N\setminus C$ is a set of finite perimeter, $E_j\subset\mathbb R^N\setminus C_j$ with $|E_j|>0$, $j=1, \ldots, k$ are sets of finite perimeter.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def: asymptotic configuration}
			Let $C\subset\mathbb R^N$ be a closed convex set.
            We call the couple $(\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{C})$ an \emph{asymptotic configuration} for $C$ if there exists $k\in \mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}$ such that
            \begin{equation}
				\mathbf E=(E_0, \ldots, E_k), \qquad \mathbf C=(C,C_1, \ldots, C_k),
			\end{equation} 
            where $C_1, \ldots, C_k$ are asymptotic cylinders for $C$, $E_0\subset \mathbb R^N\setminus C$ is a set of finite perimeter, $E_j\subset\mathbb R^N\setminus C_j$ with $|E_j|>0$, $j=1, \ldots, k$ are sets of finite perimeter.
            
			We say that $(\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{C})$ has length $k$ and volume $|\mathbf E|=\sum_{j=0}^k |E_j|$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def: generalized minimizer}
			Let $C\subset\mathbb R^N$ be a closed convex set and $v>0$. 
            
            An \emph{asymptotic} (or \emph{ generalized}) \emph{minimizer} for $C$ with volume constraint $v$ is an asymptotic configuration $(\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{C})$ such that
            \begin{equation}
                \mathcal{G}_C(\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{C}) \leq \mathcal{G}_C(\tilde{\mathbf{E}}, \tilde{\mathbf{C}})
            \end{equation}
            among all of the asymptotic configurations $(\tilde{\mathbf{E}}, \tilde{\mathbf{C}})$ for $C$ with volume $v$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{teo: generalized}
			Let $C$ be a closed convex set, $\lambda\in \left(-1, 1\right)$ and $v>0$.		
			There exist a generalized minimizer $(\mathbf E, \mathbf C)$ for $C$ with volume constraint $v$. 
            Moreover:
			\begin{equation}
				\mathcal{G}_C(\mathbf E, \mathbf C)=\min \mathcal{G}_C( \tilde{\mathbf E}, \tilde{\mathbf C})=\inf \mathcal{F}_C(F),
			\end{equation}
			where $|\mathbf E|=v$ and $( \tilde{\mathbf E}, \tilde{\mathbf C})$ varies among all asymptotic configurations for $C$ of volume $v$ and $F\subset\mathbb R^N\setminus C$ ranges among all sets of finite perimeter with volume $v$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{teo: trace 1}
				Let $u\in BV(\Omega)$ and let $\Gamma \subset \Omega$ be a $\mathcal H^{N-1}$--rectifiable set oriented by $\nu$. Then, for $\mathcal H^{N-1}$--a.e. $x\in\Gamma$ there exist $\operatorname{Tr}^+_\Gamma u(x), \operatorname{Tr}^-_\Gamma u(x)$ such that
				\begin{equation}
					\lim_{\rho \to 0^+} \mathchoice
	{{\setbox0=\hbox{$\displaystyle{\textstyle-}{\int}$ }
		\vcenter{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-.6\wd0}} 
	{{\setbox0=\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle-}{\int}$ }
		\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-.6\wd0}} 
	{{\setbox0=\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\scriptscriptstyle-}{\int}$ }
		\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-.6\wd0}} 
	{{\setbox0=\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\scriptscriptstyle-}{\int}$ }
		\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-.6\wd0}} 
	\!\int_{B^\pm(x, \rho, \nu(x))}|u(y)-\operatorname{Tr}^\pm_\Gamma u(x)|\;dy=0,
				\end{equation}
				where $B^\pm(x, \rho, \nu(x))=B(x, \rho)\cap\{\pm x\cdot \nu> 0\}$.
				Moreover, $Du_{|\Gamma}=(\operatorname{Tr}^+_\Gamma u-\operatorname{Tr}^-_\Gamma u)\nu\,\mathcal H^{N-1}_{|\Gamma}$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{teo: trace 2}
				Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^N$ be an open domain with bounded Lipschitz boundary. Let $u\in BV(\Omega)$. Then, for $\mathcal H^{N-1}$--a.e. $x\in\partial\Omega$ there exists $\operatorname{Tr}_\Omega u(x)$ such that
				\begin{equation}
					\lim_{\rho \to 0^+}\mathchoice
	{{\setbox0=\hbox{$\displaystyle{\textstyle-}{\int}$ }
		\vcenter{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-.6\wd0}} 
	{{\setbox0=\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle-}{\int}$ }
		\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-.6\wd0}} 
	{{\setbox0=\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\scriptscriptstyle-}{\int}$ }
		\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-.6\wd0}} 
	{{\setbox0=\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\scriptscriptstyle-}{\int}$ }
		\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-.6\wd0}} 
	\!\int_{B(x, \rho)\cap\Omega }|u(y)-\operatorname{Tr}_\Omega u(x)|\;dy=0.
				\end{equation}
				The trace operator which sends $u$ to $\operatorname{Tr}_\Omega u$ is continuous from $BV(\Omega)$ to $L^1(\partial \Omega)$ where the first is endowed with the topology of strict convergence and the second with the strong topology.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
If $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^N$ is an open domain with bounded Lipschitz boundary, $\partial \Omega$ is a rectifiable set oriented by the exterior normal $\nu$.
                In this case, if $u\in BV(\mathbb R^N)$, then $u_{|\Omega}\in BV(\Omega)$  and $\operatorname{Tr}_\Omega  u_{|\Omega} =\operatorname{Tr}^-_{\partial \Omega} u$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
Let $E\subset\mathbb R^N$ be a set of (locally) finite perimeter. Then there exist a (locally) $\mathcal H^{N-1}$--rectifiable set $\partial^* E\subset \partial E$, called \emph{reduced boundary} of $E$, and a function $\nu_E:\partial^* E\to\mathbb{S}^{N-1}$, called \emph{outward generalized normal}, such that:
				\begin{itemize}
					\item the polar decomposition of $\mu_E$ reads as
					\begin{equation}
						\mu_E=-\nu_E\; \mathcal H^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}\partial^* E;
					\end{equation}
					\item $\nu_E (x)\perp T_x \partial^* E$ for $\mathcal H^{N-1}$ every $x$ in $\partial^* E$;
					\item $\lim\limits_{r\to 0^+}\left(\frac{E-x}{r}\right)= \{y\in \mathbb{R}^N \, : \, y\cdot \nu_E(x)<0\}$ in $L^1_{loc}$ for every $x\in\partial^* E$.
				\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
Let $E$ be a Borel set. For $\alpha\in[0, 1]$, we write
                \begin{equation}
                    E^{(\alpha)}\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathbb R^N:\; \lim_{r\to 0^+} \frac{|E\cap B(x, r)|}{|B(x, r)|}=\alpha\right\}.
                \end{equation}
                
                The essential boundary of $E$ is defined as $\partial^e E\coloneqq \mathbb{R}^N\setminus (E^{(0)}\cup E^{(1)})$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{teo: Federer}
				Let $E$ be a set of locally finite perimeter. Then
				\begin{equation}
					\mathcal H^{N-1}(\partial^e E\setminus \partial ^*E)=0.
				\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->

<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lemma: local bulk wet estimate}
				Let $C\subset \mathbb R^N$ be a set of locally finite perimeter such that $\mathcal H^{N-1}(\partial C\setminus \partial^* C)=0$. 
                Then for all $\eta, \delta>0$ there exists $m=m\left(\eta, \delta, C\right)>0$ such that for all $E\subset \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$ finite perimeter sets it holds
				\begin{equation}
					P\left(E; \partial^* C\right)\leq P\left(E; I_\delta\right)+m|E\cap I_\delta|+\eta,
				\end{equation}
				where $I_\delta=\{x \in \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C:\; 0 < d\left(x, C\right)<\delta \}$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Let $\eta, \delta>0$ and $C, E$ as above. 
            Let $R>0$, by \eqref{eq: perimetro} and a cut-off argument, there exists $T\in C^{\infty}_c\left(B(0,R);\mathbb R^N\right)$ such that $|T|\leq 1$, $\int_{\partial^*C \cap B(0,R)}\left(1+T\cdot \nu_C\right)\, d\mathcal{H}^{N-1}<\eta$ and $\text{supp}( T)\subset \bar{C}\cup I_\delta$. 
            By choosing $m=\|\nabla T\|_\infty$ and recalling $\mathcal H^{N-1}(\partial C\setminus \partial^* C)=0$, we get
			\begin{equation}
					\int_E \operatorname{div}T\, dx\leq m |E\cap I_\delta|. 
			\end{equation}
			On the other hand, by the Gauss--Green formula 
			\begin{align}
				\int_E \operatorname{div}T\, dx&= \int_{\partial^*E\cap \partial^*C \cap B(0,R)}  T\cdot \nu_E\;d\mathcal{H}^{N-1}
				+ \int_{\partial^*E\setminus \bar{C}} T\cdot \nu_E\;d\mathcal{H}^{N-1} \\
				&\geq P\left(E;\partial^* C\cap B(0,R)\right)-\eta-P\left(E;I_\delta\right).
			\end{align}
            Putting everything together and letting $R\to +\infty$ yields the desired inequality.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lemma: local bulk wet estimate N-1}
			Let $C\subset \{x_N=0\}$ be a bounded set 
                                                   
            such that $\mathcal H^{N-1}(\partial C\cap C)=0$, where by $\partial C$ we mean the boundary of $C$ in $\{x_N=0\}$. 
            Then for all $\eta, \delta>0$ there exists $m=m\left(\eta, \delta, C\right)>0$ such that for all sets $E\subset \mathbb{R}^N$ of finite perimeter it holds
				\begin{equation}\label{eq: local bulk wet estimate N-1}
                \int_C {\rm Tr}^+_C \mathbbm{1}_E\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}\leq P(E; C\times (0, \delta))+m|E\cap\; C\times(0, \delta)|+\eta.
            \end{equation}
            The same holds replacing ${\rm Tr}^+_C$ with ${\rm Tr}^-_C$ and $C\times (0, \delta)$ with $C\times (-\delta, 0)$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
The proof is similar to the one of \Cref{lemma: local bulk wet estimate}. Let $\eta, \delta>0$ and $C, E$ as above.

            Let us start by considering $F=E\cap \{x_N\geq 0\}$.
            Notice that ${\rm Tr}^+_C \mathbbm{1}_E={\rm Tr}^+_C \mathbbm{1}_F$, $P(E; C\times (0, \delta)) = P(F; C\times (0, \delta))$ and $|E\cap\; C\times(0, \delta)|=|F\cap\; C\times(0, \delta)|$.
            In particular, it is enough to prove the statement for $F$.

            Fix $\sigma>0$.
            Let $C_\sigma=\{x\in C: d(x, \{x_N=0\}\setminus C)>\sigma\}$ and notice that, by assumption, $\mathcal H^{N-1}(C_\sigma)\to \mathcal H^{N-1}(C)$ as $\sigma\to 0$.
            Let also $\varphi_\sigma\in C^1_c(C\times (-\delta, \delta))$ be such that $-1\leq \varphi_\sigma\leq 0$ and $\varphi\equiv-1$ on $C_\sigma$.
            Finally, let $e_N$ be the $N$-th versor. By the Gauss--Green formula 
			\begin{align}
				\int_F \operatorname{div}(\varphi_\sigma e_N)\, dx&= -\int_{\partial^*F\cap C}  \varphi_\sigma \;d\mathcal{H}^{N-1}
				+ \int_{\partial^* F\setminus C} \varphi_\sigma \,(e_N\cdot \nu_F)\;d\mathcal{H}^{N-1} \\
				&\geq \int_{C_\sigma} {\rm Tr}^+_C \mathbbm{1}_F\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}-P\left(F;C\times(0, \delta)\right).
			\end{align}
            On the other hand, 
            \begin{equation}
                \int_F \operatorname{div}(\varphi_\sigma e_N)\, dx\leq \|\nabla \varphi_\sigma\|_\infty |F\cap \,C\times (-\delta, \delta)|=\|\nabla \varphi_\sigma\|_\infty |F\cap \,C\times (0, \delta)|.
            \end{equation}

            Taking $\sigma$ small enough that
            \begin{equation}
                \int_{C_\sigma} {\rm Tr}^+_C \mathbbm{1}_F\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}\geq \int_{C}{\rm Tr}^+_C \mathbbm{1}_F\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}-\eta
            \end{equation}
            concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop: bulk contact inequality}
			Let $C\subset \mathbb R^N$ be a convex body and $E \subset \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$ a set with finite measure and finite perimeter. Then
			\begin{equation}
				P\left(E; \partial C\right)\leq P\left(E; \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C\right)
			\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rmk: bulk contact inequality in the case N-1}
			A proof different from the one in \cite{FuMo23} can be obtained in the case of regular sets by noticing that $T=\nabla d(C, \cdot)$ is an extension of the outer normal of $C$ and has positive divergence: integrating by parts $\operatorname{div} T$ in $E$ yields the inequality. By approximation (for instance, with $C^\eta\coloneqq \{d(x, C)<\eta\}$), the inequality can be shown to hold for general $C$'s. In particular, if $C$ has dimension $N-1$ (and we may assume $C\subset \{x_N=0\}$), it can be strengthened to 
			\begin{equation}
				\int_C \operatorname{Tr}_{C}^+ \mathbbm{1}_E \, d\mathcal H^{N-1}\leq P(E; \{x_N>0\}), 
			\end{equation} 
			where we assume that $C$ is oriented by $e_N$.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor: coercivity}
            Let $C$ be a closed convex set.
            Then for every set of finite perimeter $E\subset \mathbb R^N\setminus C$ it holds
            \begin{equation}
                \mathcal{F}_C(E)\geq \frac{1-\lambda}{2} P(E).
            \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop: semicontinuity}
			Let $C$ be a compact convex set. Let $\left(E_n\right)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of sets of finite perimeter in $\mathbb R^N$ such that $E_n\to E$ in $L^1$ for some set $E\subset \mathbb R^N$. Then
			\begin{equation}
				\mathcal{F}_C \left(E\right)\leq \liminf_n \mathcal{F}_C \left(E_n\right)
			\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let first $C$ be a convex body, i.e.\ $\dim C=N$.
            Let $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ and $E$ be as in the statement. We can assume that $ \liminf\limits_n \mathcal{F}_C \left(E_n\right) <+\infty$ and that $E$ has finite perimeter.
            Fix $\delta, \eta>0$ and set $I_\delta\coloneqq \{x \in \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C:\;  d\left(x, C\right)<\delta \}$.
            Notice that, by Lemma \ref{lemma: local bulk wet estimate},
            \begin{align}
              |P(E_n; \partial C)-P(E;\partial C)|&\leq P(E\Delta E_n; \partial C)\\
              &\leq P(E\Delta E_n; I_\delta)+m|E\Delta E_n\cap I_\delta|+\eta.
            \end{align}
            In particular, 
            \begin{align}
                \mathcal F_C(E_n)-\mathcal F_C(E)&\geq P(E_n, \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C)-P(E, \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C)-\lambda\left( P(E_n; \partial C)-P(E;\partial C) \right)\\
                &\geq P(E_n; \mathbb R^N\setminus \@ifnextchar^{{

            \end{equation}
            Now, sending $\delta\to0$ and $\eta\to 0$, it follows
            \begin{equation}
                \mathcal F_C(E)\leq \liminf_n \mathcal F_C(E_n).
            \end{equation}

            If $\dim C<N-1$, the thesis follows from the semicontinuity of the perimeter.

            Finally, the case $\dim C=N-1$ follows as in the previous case, by applying \Cref{lemma: local bulk wet estimate N-1} instead of \Cref{lemma: local bulk wet estimate}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor: esistenza}
			Let $C$ be a closed convex set and let $R>0$. 
            Then there exists $E_R\subset B\left(0, R\right)\setminus C$ such that
			\begin{equation}\label{eq: minimi locali}
				\mathcal F_{C, \Lambda}^{v}\left(E_R\right)=\min \{\mathcal F_{C, \Lambda}^{v}\left(E\right): \; E\subset B\left(0, R \right) \setminus C\}  .
			\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
Let $C$ be a closed convex set and 
            let $E\subset \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C$.
            For $R>0$ we say that $E$ is a \emph{$\Lambda$-minimizer of $\mathcal{F}_C$ in $B(0,R)$} if $E\subset B(0, R)$ and
            \begin{align}
            \mathcal{F}_C\left(E\right)\leq \mathcal{F}_C\left(F\right)+\Lambda|E\Delta F| \label{eq: lambda minimality in B(0,R)}
            \end{align}
            holds for all $F\subset B(0,R)\setminus C$.
            In general, we say that $E$ is a \emph{$\Lambda$-minimizer of $\mathcal{F}_C$} if for every open ball $B$
            \begin{equation}
                \mathcal{F}_C\left(E, B\right)\leq \mathcal{F}_C\left(F, B\right)+\Lambda|E\Delta F| \label{eq: lambda minimality}
            \end{equation}  
            holds for every set $F \subset \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C$ such that $F \Delta E \Subset B$.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop: density est}
        Let $C$ be a closed convex set and
        let $E\subset \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$ be such that it is either a $\Lambda$-minimizer of $\mathcal{F}_C$ or a $\Lambda$-minimizer of $\mathcal{F}_C$ in $B(0,R)$ for an $R>0$.
        Then  
        \begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
            \item\label{it: regularity min} $E$ is $\mathcal L^N$--equivalent to the open set $\Omega= E^{(1)} \subset \mathbb R^N$ that satisfies $\partial \Omega=\partial^e\Omega$.
            \item\label{it: density est} There exist $c_0=c_0\left(\lambda\right)>0, r_0=r_0\left(\lambda, \Lambda\right)\in\left(0, 1\right)$ such that if $\Omega'$ is any connected component of $\Omega$, one has \begin{equation} \label{eq: density estimates 2}
                |\Omega'\cap B\left(x, r\right)|\geq c_0 r^N
            \end{equation}
            for all $x\in \@ifnextchar^{{

The proof of the first two items is identical to that of \cite[Lemma 3.6]{FoFuLeMo23}. The case $\dim(C)=N-1$ is treated exactly as the others, except that one has to use the strengthened version of \Cref{prop: bulk contact inequality} stated in \Cref{rmk: bulk contact inequality in the case N-1}.

}(x, r)\cap C=\emptyset$ and consider $F=E\setminus B(x, r)$. 
        Since the right-hand side of the inequality in point \ref{it: perim est} is continuous in $r$ and the left-hand side lower semicontinuous, by foliaton lemma it is enough to prove the statement in the hypothesis that $P(E; \partial B(x, r))=0$.
        In particular, 
        \begin{align}
            \mathcal{F}_C\left(F\right)&=\mathcal{F}_C\left(E\right)-P(E; B(x, r))+P(B(x, r); E^{(1)})\\
            &\leq \mathcal{F}_C\left(E\right)-P(E; B(x, r))+N\omega_N r^{N-1}.
        \end{align}
        Plugging the estimate in \eqref{eq: lambda minimality in B(0,R)}, we get
        \begin{equation}
            P(E; B(x, r))\leq N\omega_N r^{N-1}+\Lambda |E\Delta F|\leq (N+\Lambda) \omega_N r^{N-1}.
        \end{equation}

        Finally, let us prove item \ref{it: height est}.

}'$ and $H=\sup\limits_{z\in \Omega'\cap B(x, r)} d(z, C)$.
        Notice that, by the density estimates, 
        \begin{equation}\label{eq: misura intersezione}
            c_0 r^N\leq |\Omega'\cap B(x, r)|\leq |B(x, r)\cap C^{H}|, 
        \end{equation}
        where $C^H=\{z\in \mathbb R^N\setminus C:0\leq d(z, C)\leq H\}$.
        Let us estimate $|B(x, r)\cap C^{H}|$.
        By the Coarea formula, 
        \begin{equation}
            |B(x, r)\cap C^{H}|=\int_0^H \mathcal H^{N-1}\left(\{z\in B(x, r):d(z, C)=s\}\right)\,ds.
        \end{equation}
        Now 
        \begin{equation}
            \{z\in B(x, r):d(z, C)=s\}\subset \partial \{z\in B(x, r): d(z, C)\leq s\}\eqqcolon \partial C_s
        \end{equation}
        for all $s\in [0, H]$.
        Moreover, the sets $C_s$ are convex, so by Proposition \ref{prop: bulk contact inequality}, 
        \begin{equation}
            \mathcal H^{N-1}(\{z\in B(x, r):d(z, C)=s\})\leq \mathcal H^{N-1}(\partial C_s)\leq \mathcal{H}^{N-1}(\partial B(x, r))= N\omega_Nr^{N-1}.
        \end{equation}
        In particular, by \eqref{eq: misura intersezione} we get
        \begin{equation}
            H\geq \frac{c_0 r}{N\omega_N}=\frac{c_0 r_0}{2 N\omega_N}\eqqcolon h.
        \end{equation}
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rmk: componenti connesse e scelta del rappresentante}
    By \cite[Theorem 2]{AmbrosioCaselles}, If $E \subset \mathbb{R}^N$ is such that $E^{(1)}$ is open and $\partial E^{(1)} = \partial^e E$, then the $M$-connected components (see \cite[Definition 1]{AmbrosioCaselles} for the definition) of $E$ coincides with the (topological) connected components of $E^{(1)}$. Hence, by \Cref{prop: density est}, if $E \subset \mathbb{R}^N$ is a $\Lambda$-minimizer of $\mathcal{F}_C$, we can unambiguously refer to the connected components of $E$ as those of its representative $E^{(1)}$. Moreover, in the rest of the paper, unless otherwise specified, whenever $E$ is a $\Lambda$-minimizer of $\mathcal{F}_C$, we will always choose $E^{(1)}$ as its precise representative.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor: stima sulle comp connesse e loro diametro}
                Let $v>0$ and $\Lambda>0$. There exist $M=M(\lambda, \Lambda, v)>0$ and $d=d(\lambda, \Lambda, v)>0$ such that if $C$ is a closed convex set, $R>0$ and $E\subset\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$ is a minimizer of  $\mathcal{F}^{v}_{C,\Lambda}$ among subsets of $B(0,R) \setminus C$ (or among subsets of $\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$) then $E$ that has less than $M$ connected components and every connected component has diameter less than $d$.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->

<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem: Ivanov}
Let $C$ be a convex body. Then, for $\mathcal{L}^N$-a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^N$, and for every line $L$ passing through $x$, one has $\mathcal{H}^1(L\cap \partial C)=0$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop: misura bordo conv e conv dilatato}
Let $C$ be a convex body. Then, for $\mathcal{L}^N$-a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^N$, there exists a countable set $S\subset (0,+\infty)$ such that
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}\bigl(\partial(\lambda (C-x)) \cap \partial (C-x)\bigr)=0
\end{equation}
for every $\lambda\in (0,+\infty)\setminus S$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We distinguish the cases $x\in {\rm int}(C)$ and $x\notin C$. Assume first that $x\in {\rm int}(C)$. In this case, by convexity, the family
\begin{equation}
\bigl(\partial(\lambda (C-x))\bigr)_{\lambda>0}
\end{equation}
is pairwise disjoint. Hence, by the foliation lemma,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}\bigl(\partial(\lambda (C-x))\cap \partial(C-x)\bigr)=0
\end{equation}
for every $\lambda>0$ except at most countably many values of $\lambda$.

Assume now that $x\in \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$. By \Cref{lem: Ivanov}, for $\mathcal{L}^N$-a.e. such $x$, every line $L$ passing through $x$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^1(L\cap \partial C)=0.
\end{equation}
Let $x$ be such that previous property is satisfied, since $C$ is convex, every line through $x$ intersects $\partial C$ in at most two points. We then decompose $\partial C$ into the following three disjoint sets:
\begin{equation}
C_1:=\Bigl \{ y\in \partial C \ :\ \exists \ \nu\in \mathbb{S}^{N-1}, \ \exists \ 0<t<s
\ \text{such that } y=x+t\nu \text{ and } x+s\nu\in \partial C \Bigr \},
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
C_2:=\Bigl \{ y\in \partial C \ :\ \exists \ \nu\in \mathbb{S}^{N-1}, \ \exists \ 0<t<s
\ \text{such that } y=x+s\nu \text{ and } x+t\nu\in \partial C \Bigr \},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{align}
C_3:=\Bigl \{ y\in \partial C \ :\ &\exists \ \nu\in \mathbb{S}^{N-1}, \ \text{such that } (x+\mathbb{R}\nu)\cap \partial C= \{y\} \Bigr \}.
\end{align}
Thus, $C_1$ and $C_2$ consist respectively of the first and second intersection points of rays starting from $x$ with $\partial C$, while $C_3$ contains the points corresponding to rays having a unique intersection with $\partial C$. Fix $\lambda>0$ with $\lambda\neq 1$. By construction,
\begin{equation}
\lambda(C_1-x)\cap (C_1-x)=\emptyset,
\qquad
\lambda(C_2-x)\cap (C_2-x)=\emptyset, 
\end{equation}
and moreover
\begin{equation}
\lambda(C_3-x)\cap \partial(C-x)=\emptyset,
\end{equation}
since points of $C_3$ correspond to rays intersecting $\partial C$ at exactly one point. In the case $0<\lambda<1$ (the case $\lambda>1$ being analogous), we also have, again by construction, that
\begin{equation}
\lambda(C_1-x)\cap (C_2-x)=\emptyset,
\end{equation}
Therefore, if
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}\bigl(\partial(\lambda(C-x))\cap \partial(C-x)\bigr)>0,
\end{equation}
then necessarily
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}\bigl(\lambda(C_2-x)\cap (C_1-x)\bigr)>0.
\end{equation}
On the other hand, the family
\begin{equation}
\bigl(\lambda(C_2-x)\bigr)_{0<\lambda<1}
\end{equation}
is pairwise disjoint. Hence, applying once again the foliation lemma, we conclude that
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}\bigl(\lambda(C_2-x)\cap (C_1-x)\bigr)=0
\end{equation}
for every $\lambda\in(0,1)$ except at most countably many values. This concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lm: quasi ovunque disgiunti}
Let $(C_k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of convex bodies. For $\mathcal{L}^N$-a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^N$ there exists a set $A\subset (0,+\infty)$ such that for every $\lambda,\mu\in A$, with $\lambda\neq\mu$, one has
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}\bigl(\lambda(\partial C_k - x)\cap \mu(\partial C_k - x)\bigr)=0
\end{equation}
for every $k\in\mathbb{N}$. Moreover, for every $\delta>0$ the set $A\cap (1-\delta,1)$ is uncountable.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
As a consequence of \Cref{prop: misura bordo conv e conv dilatato}, for $\mathcal{L}^N$-a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^N$ there exists a countable set $S\subset (0,+\infty)$ such that
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}\bigl(\lambda(\partial C_k-x)\cap \partial (C_k-x)\bigr)=0
\end{equation}
for every $k\in\mathbb{N}$ and every $\lambda\in (0,+\infty)\setminus S$. Fix such an $x \in\mathbb{R}^N$ and the corresponding set $S$.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that $\mathbb{Q}^+\subset S$. Let $\Gamma$ be the multiplicative subgroup of $(0,+\infty)$ generated by $S$, namely
\begin{equation}
\Gamma=\left\{\frac{t_1\cdots t_l}{s_1\cdots s_m}:\; l,m\in \mathbb{N}, \, \, t_i,s_j\in S, \, \, i=1,\dots, l \, \, j= 1, \dots, m\right\}.
\end{equation}
Since $S$ is countable, also $\Gamma$ is countable.

We define an equivalence relation on $(0,+\infty)$ by
\begin{equation}
\lambda \sim \mu
\qquad \Longleftrightarrow \qquad
\frac{\lambda}{\mu}\in \Gamma.
\end{equation}

Each equivalence class is countable and dense in $(0,+\infty)$.

If $\mu \notin [\lambda]_{\sim}$, then $\frac{\lambda}{\mu}\notin S$, hence 
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}\bigl(\lambda(\partial C_k-x)\cap \mu(\partial C_k -x)\bigr) =0
\end{equation}
for every $k\in\mathbb{N}$.

Let $L$ denote the set of equivalence classes. Since $(0,+\infty)$ has cardinality $2^{\aleph_0}$ and each class is countable, $L$ has cardinality $2^{\aleph_0}$. Hence we can write
\begin{equation}
L=\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}} L_n
\end{equation}
where the union is disjoint and each $L_n$ has cardinality $2^{\aleph_0}$.

For every $n\in\mathbb{N}$ and for every equivalence class $[\lambda]_{\sim}\in L_n$, choose a representative of the class $[\lambda]_{\sim}$ in the interval
$\left(1- \frac{1}{n},\,1 \right)$,
which is possible since each equivalence class is dense. Let $A_n$ denote the set of all representatives chosen in this way. Then
\begin{equation}
A_n \subset \left(1-\frac{1}{n},\,1 \right),
\qquad |A_n|=2^{\aleph_0}.
\end{equation}

Finally, set
\begin{equation}
A=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}} A_n.
\end{equation}
By construction, $A$ is the desired set.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lm: approssimazione}
				Let $(C_k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of convex bodies and let $E\subset\mathbb R^N\setminus C_1$ be a set of finite perimeter with $|E|<+\infty$. Then there exist a sequence of bounded open sets with $C^\infty$ boundary $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ such that
				\begin{itemize}
					\item $E_n\setminus C_1\xrightarrow{n\to +\infty} E$ strictly;
					\item $P(E_n; \partial C_k)=0$ for every $n,k \in \mathbb{N}$;
					\item $\operatorname{Tr}_{\partial C_1}^+ \mathbbm{1}_{E_n}\xrightarrow{n\to +\infty} \operatorname{Tr}_{\partial C_1}^+ \mathbbm{1}_{E}$ in $L^1(\partial C_1, \mathcal H^{N-1})$.
				\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
By \Cref{lm: quasi ovunque disgiunti} there exists $x\in{\rm int}(C_1)$ and a set $A\subset (0,+\infty)$ such that the conclusions of the previous Lemma hold.  
                Without loss of generality we can assume $x=0$.
				By standard arguments (considering the intersection with balls of diverging radii), we can also assume that there exists $R>0$ such that $E\Subset B(0, R)$.
				
				\textbf{Step 1:} Approximation with sets $F$ such that $P(F, \partial C_k)=0$ for every $k\in\mathbb{N}$.\\
				Let us set, for $\lambda\in(0, 1)$, $F_\lambda=\lambda E$. It is immediate to see that $F_\lambda\to E$ strictly as $\lambda \to 1$. Let us show that there exist a sequence $\lambda_n\to 1^{-}$ such that
                \begin{equation}
                    P(F_{\lambda_n}; \partial C_k)=0 \ \text{for every} \ n,k\in\mathbb{N} \label{eq: foliation nel lemma di approx}
                \end{equation} 
                Indeed, since for every $k\in\mathbb{N}$ the family $(\frac{1}{\lambda} \partial C_k)_{\lambda \in A}$ is $\mathcal{H}^{N-1}$-pairwise disjoint, by the Foliation Lemma we have that there exist a countable set $B \subset A$ such that $P(E, \frac{1}{\lambda} \partial C_k) = 0$ for every $k\in\mathbb{N}$ and every $\lambda \in A \setminus B$.
                By \Cref{lm: quasi ovunque disgiunti}, $1$ is an accumulation point of $A \setminus B$, hence we can find a sequence $\lambda_n \to 1^{-}$ such that \eqref{eq: foliation nel lemma di approx} holds.
                
                Notice moreover that $\mathbbm{1}_{F_{\lambda_n}\setminus C_1}\to \mathbbm{1}_E$ strictly in $BV(\mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_1)$. Indeed,
                \begin{equation}
                P(F_{\lambda_n}, \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_1)
                = \lambda_n^{N-1} P\!\left(E, \mathbb{R}^N \setminus \frac{1}{\lambda_n} C_1 \right)
                = \int \lambda_n^{N-1}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^N \setminus (\frac{1}{\lambda_n} C_1)} \, d|\mu_E|.
                \end{equation}
                
                Since the integrand converges pointwise to $\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_1}$ and $P(E) < +\infty$, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem we obtain
                \begin{equation}
                P(F_{\lambda_n}, \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_1) \to P(E, \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_1).
                \end{equation}
                
                Therefore, \Cref{teo: trace 2} yields
                \begin{equation}
                \operatorname{Tr}_{\partial C_1}^{+}\mathbbm{1}_{F_{\lambda_n}}
                \to
                \operatorname{Tr}_{\partial C_1}^{+}\mathbbm{1}_E
                \qquad \text{in } L^1(\partial C_1,\mathcal H^{N-1}).
                \end{equation}
                
                In particular, this also implies that
                \begin{equation}
                P(F_{\lambda_n} \setminus C_1)\to P(E).
                \end{equation}
				
			\textbf{Step 2:} Approximation by smooth sets.\\
				It is now sufficient to approximate a set $F$ such that $P(F;\partial C_k)=0$ for every $k\in\mathbb{N}$ and $F\Subset B(0,R)$ for some $R>0$.
            By the standard theory of sets of finite perimeter, there exists a sequence $(F_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ of open sets with smooth boundary such that $F_n\to F$ strictly in $BV$ and $F_n\Subset B(0,R)$ for every $n\in \mathbb N$. 
            By the same argument as in Step 1, we may assume that $P(F_n;\partial C_k)=0$ for all $n,k\in\mathbb{N}$. Since $F_n \to F$ in $L^1$, we have $\mu_{F_n}\xrightharpoonup{*}\mu_F$.
            Moreover, since $P(F_n)\to P(F)<+\infty$, by \cite[Proposition 4.30]{Ma12} we obtain $|\mu_{F_n}|\xrightharpoonup{*}|\mu_F|$. Since $P(F,\partial C_1)=0$, by \cite[Proposition 4.26]{Ma12} it follows that
            $
            P(F_n,\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C_1)\to P(F,\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C_1).
            $
            Hence $\mathbbm{1}_{F_n\setminus C_1}\to \mathbbm{1}_{F\setminus C_1}$ strictly in $BV(\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C_1)$, which, by \Cref{teo: trace 2}, yields the conclusion.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
Let $(F_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of sets in $\mathbb R^N$. We say that $F_n$ converges in the sense of Kuratowski to a (possibly empty) set $F$ if
				\begin{itemize}
					\item Every point $x\in F$ is the limit of a sequence $(x_n)_{n\geq1}$ such that $x_n\in F_n$ eventually,
					\item any limit point of any sequence $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$, with $x_n\in F_n$, must be in $F$.
				\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
Let $(F_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of closed sets. Then there exists some subsequence which converges in the Kuratowski sense.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lemma: local hausdorff}
				Let $F\subset\mathbb R^N$ a closed set and $(F_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of closed sets in $\mathbb R^N$ such that $F_n\to F$ in the sense of Kuratowski.
				Let $G$ be a bounded open set such that $F\cap G\neq \emptyset$ and assume that $\@ifnextchar^{{ 
	\setbox0\hbox{${\save@mathaccent"0362{F\cap G}}^H$} 
	\setbox2\hbox{${\save@mathaccent"0362{\kern0ptF\cap G}}^H$} 
	\ifdim\ht0=\ht2  
	\begingroup
	\def\mathaccent#F\cap G#0{ 
		                           
		\let\mathaccent\save@mathaccent
		                                                                                              
		\if22 \let \fi
		                                 
		\setbox\z@\hbox{$\macc@style{}_{}$} 
		\setbox\tw@\hbox{$\macc@style{}{}_{}$} 
		\dimen@\wd\tw@
		\advance\dimen@-\wd\z@
		                                                    
		\divide\dimen@ 3
		\@tempdima\wd\tw@
		\advance\@tempdima-\scriptspace
		                                           
		\divide\@tempdima 10
		\advance\dimen@-\@tempdima
		                                                                            
		\ifdim\dimen@>\z@ \dimen@0pt\fi
		                                                  
		\kern0.6\dimexpr\macc@kerna\kern-\dimen@
		\if21
		\overline{\kern-0.6\dimexpr\macc@kerna\kern\dimen@\kern0.4\dimexpr\macc@kerna\kern\dimen@} 
		\advance\dimen@0.4\dimexpr\macc@kerna
		                                                                                             
		\let\final@kern0 
		\ifdim\dimen@<\z@ \let\final@kern1\fi
		\if\final@kern1 \kern-\dimen@\fi
		\else
		\overline{\kern-0.6\dimexpr\macc@kerna\kern\dimen@F\cap G} 
		\fi
	} 
	\macc@depth\@ne
	\let\@empty\@empty \let\macc@set@skewchar\macc@set@skewchar
	\mathsurround\z@ \frozen@everymath{\mathgroup\macc@group\relax} 
	\macc@set@skewchar\relax
	\let\macc@nested@a\macc@nested@a
	                                                             
	\if21
	\macc@nested@a\relax111{F\cap G} 
	\else
	                                                                            
	                                                
	\def\gobble@till@marker#F\cap G\endmarker{} 
	\futurelet\gobble@till@markerF\cap G\endmarker
	\ifcat\noexpand A\else
	\def{}

<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
Let $C\subset\mathbb R^N$ be an unbounded closed convex set and let $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}\subset C$ be a diverging sequence. Then, modulo subsequences, there exists a closed convex set $K$ such that $C-x_n\to K$ in the Kuratowski sense. Moreover, $K$ contains a line and thus it is a convex cylinder.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def: asymptotic cylinders}
				Let $C\subset\mathbb R^N$ be a closed convex set.
                We say that $K\subset \mathbb R^N$ is an \emph{asymptotic cylinder} for $C$ if there exists $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}\subset C$ such that $|x_n|\to +\infty$ and $C-x_n\to K$ in the sense of Kuratowski.
                We denote by $\mathcal{K}(C)$ the set of asymptotic cylinders of $C$.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{es: loss of dim}
				Let $Q\coloneqq\{(x, y, z)\in\mathbb R^3:\, x^2+y^2\leq 1 , \;z\geq 0\}$ the unitary half cylinder and $P\coloneqq \{(x, y, z)\in\mathbb R^3:\, z=(x-1)^2 ,\; y=0,\; x\geq 1\}$ a parabolic curve. Let $C$ be the closed convex envelope of $Q\cup P$. Let us fix a diverging sequence $(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}\subset [1, +\infty)$ and set $p_n=(x_n, 0, (x_n-1)^2)$ the point on $P$ with abscissa $x_n$ for all $n\in \mathbb N$.
				Then any asymptotic cylinder of $C$ defined by subsequences of $p_n$ must have dimension at most $2$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rmk: convergenza convessi}
				Let $C_n$ be a sequence of convex bodies and $C$ be a convex body. Then $C_n\to C$ in the sense of Kuratowski if and only if $\mathbbm{1}_{C_n}\to \mathbbm{1}_{C}$ pointwise a.e.. Moreover, if this holds, $|\mu_{C_n}|=\mathcal H^{N-1} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} \partial C_n \xrightharpoonup{*} |\mu_{C}|  =\mathcal H^{N-1} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} \partial C$ in duality with $C^0_c(\mathbb R^N)$. In particular, if the sets are equibounded, $\mathbbm{1}_{C_n}\to \mathbbm{1}_{C}$ strictly.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop: i cilindri asint dei cilindri asint sono cilindri asint}
    Let $C$ be a closed convex set. If $K \in \mathcal{K}(C)$ and $L \in \mathcal{K}(K)$, then $L \in \mathcal{K}(C)$.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Throughout the proof, all limits of sets are taken in the Kuratowski sense. The argument is a standard diagonalization. We can find a diverging sequence $(x_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ in $C$ and a diverging sequence $(y_m)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}$ in $K$ such that
    \begin{equation}
        K = \lim_{n \to +\infty} (C - x_n) \, \, \, \, \, \text{and} \, \, \, \, \, L = \lim_{m \to +\infty} (K - y_m).
    \end{equation}
    Since the collection of closed sets equipped with the Kuratowski topology is first countable, we can find $(V_k)_{k \in \mathbb{N}}$, a countable basis of neighborhoods of $L$. For every $k \in \mathbb{N}$, we can find a strictly increasing sequence of indices $(m_k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}$ such that $K - y_{m_k} \in V_k.$ By the convergence $C - x_n \to K$, we can then choose a strictly increasing sequence of indices $(n_k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}$ such that
    $C - x_{n} - y_{m_k} \in V_k$ for every $n \geq n_k$. Since $y_{m_k} \in K = \lim\limits_{l \to +\infty} (C - x_{n_l})$, for every $k$ there exists a sequence $(z_l^k)_{l\in\mathbb{N}}$ in $C$ such that $y_{m_k} = \lim\limits_{l \to +\infty} (z_l^k - x_{n_l}).$  By a diagonal argument, we can find a subsequence $(n_{l_k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}$ of indices such that, letting $z_k = z^{k}_{l_k}$, we have
    \begin{align}
        z_k - (x_{n_{l_k}} + y_{m_k}) \to 0. \label{eq1: diagonalizzazione conv kuratowski}
    \end{align}
    Since the Kuratowski topology is Hausdorff and $C - x_{n_{l_k}} - y_{m_k} \in V_k$ for every $k$, we have
    \begin{align}
        L = \lim_{k \to +\infty} (C - x_{n_{l_k}} - y_{m_k}). \label{eq2: diagonalizzazione conv kuratowski}
    \end{align}
    
    Using \eqref{eq1: diagonalizzazione conv kuratowski} and \eqref{eq2: diagonalizzazione conv kuratowski}, we end up with $C - z_k \to L$, which proves that $L \in \mathcal{K}(C)$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lemma: aggiustamento convessi}
				Let $C\subset\mathbb{R}^N$ be a bounded convex body such that $0\in {\rm int}(C)$. Let $(C_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of convex bodies such that
				$C_n\to C$ in the Hausdorff metric.
				Then there exists a sequence $(\varepsilon_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ such that:
				\begin{itemize}
					\item $\varepsilon_n\to 0$;
					\item $(1-\varepsilon_n) C_n\Subset {\rm int}(C)$ eventually;
					\item $C\Subset (1+\varepsilon_n)C_n$ eventually.
				\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
By the Hausdorff convergence and convexity, there exists $r>0$ such that
				\begin{equation}
					B(0,r) \Subset {\rm int}(C) \quad \text{and} \quad B(0, r) \Subset {\rm int}(C_n)\subset C_n 
				\end{equation}
				eventually.
				By the definition of Hausdorff convergence, there exist an infinitesimal sequence $(a_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ such that
				\begin{equation}
					C_n\Subset C+B(0, a_n)\subset \left( 1+\frac{a_n}{r}\right)C
				\end{equation}
				eventually and 
				\begin{equation}
					C\Subset C_n+ B(0, a_n) \subset \left( 1+\frac{a_n}{r}\right)C_n
				\end{equation}
				eventually.
				By setting $\varepsilon_n=\max\left\{\frac{a_n}{r} , 1-\left( 1+\frac{a_n}{r}\right)^{-1}\right\}$, the thesis follows.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
Let $C$ be a bounded convex body and let $(C_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$, be a sequence of convex bodies converging to $C$ in the Hausdorff metric. Then the following statements hold:
				\begin{itemize}
					\item Let $E\subset\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$ be a set of finite perimeter such that $|E|<+\infty$. Then there exists a sequence $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ of sets of finite perimeter such that $E_n\to E$ strictly, $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_n$ and 
					\begin{equation}\label{eq: limsup}
						\mathcal F_{C_n}(E_n)\to \mathcal{F}_C(E).
					\end{equation}
					Moreover, if $E\Subset B(x, R)$ for some $x\in \mathbb R^N$, $R>0$, the sets $E_n$ can be chosen to be such that $E_n\Subset B(x, R)$.
					\item Let $E\subset\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$ be a set of finite perimeter such that $|E|<+\infty$ and $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of sets of finite perimeter such that $E_n\to E$ in $L^1$ and $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_n$. Then
					\begin{equation}\label{eq: liminf}
						\mathcal F_C(E)\leq \liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal F_{C_n}(E_n).
					\end{equation}
				\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Let's start by proving the $\liminf$ inequality. 	
				Let $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of sets of finite perimeter such that $E_n\to E$ in $L^1$ and $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_n$.
                In order to more easily prove \eqref{eq: liminf}, we distinguish the case in which $\lambda$ is bigger or equal than 0 from the case in which it is smaller. 
                First, by \Cref{lm: approssimazione,lemma: aggiustamento convessi}, we may suppose that $C_n\Subset C$ and that $P(E_n;\partial C)=0$ for all $n$. 
                Let us set $F_n=E_n\setminus C$ and $\tilde{F}_n=E_n\cap C$. Notice that $F_n\to E$ in $L^1$. 
                Assume that $\lambda\geq 0$. By \Cref{prop: bulk contact inequality} on $\tilde F_n$ we get that 
				\begin{equation}
					P(E_n;\partial C_n)\leq P(\tilde F_n;\mathbb R^N\setminus C_n)=P(F_n; \partial C)+P(E_n; {\rm int}(C)\setminus C_n).
				\end{equation}
				This yields
				\begin{align}
					\mathcal F_C(F_n)&=P(E_n;\mathbb R^N\setminus C)- \lambda P(F_n;\partial C)\\
					&\leq P(E_n;\mathbb R^N\setminus C)-\lambda P(E_n;\partial C_n)+\lambda P(E_n; {\rm int}(C)\setminus C_n)\\
					&\leq P(E_n;\mathbb R^N\setminus C)+ P(E_n; {\rm int}(C)\setminus C_n)-\lambda P(E_n;\partial C_n)= \mathcal F_{C_n}(E_n).
				\end{align}
				Since the capillarity energy functional is lower semicontinuous (\Cref{prop: semicontinuity}), we get
				\begin{equation}
					\mathcal F_C(E)\leq \liminf_n \mathcal F_C(F_n)\leq \liminf_n \mathcal F_{C_n}(E_n).
				\end{equation}
				
				Finally, assume $\lambda<0$. Let us apply \Cref{lemma: local bulk wet estimate} on the sets $\tilde F_n\subset C$ with the role of the obstacle played by $\mathbb R^N\setminus C$. Fix $\delta, \eta>0$ and set $I_\delta\coloneqq \{x\in {\rm int}(C)\text{ s.t. }d(x, \mathbb R^N\setminus C)<\delta\}$.
				For $n\in \mathbb{N}$ we get:
				\begin{align}
					P(F_n; \partial C)=P(\tilde F_n; \partial C)&\leq P(E_n; I_\delta)+m(\delta, \eta)| E_n\cap C|+\eta\\
					&\leq P(E_n;\partial C_n)+P(E_n; {\rm int} (C)\setminus C_n)\\
					&+ m(\delta, \eta)|E_n\cap C|+\eta.
				\end{align}
				By semicontinuity, this yields
				\begin{align}
					\mathcal F_C(E)&\leq \liminf_n \mathcal F_C(F_n)\\
					&=\liminf_n\left[P(E_n; \mathbb R^N\setminus C)-\lambda P(F_n; \partial C)\right]\\
					&\leq \liminf_n \Big[ P(E_n; \mathbb R^N\setminus C)\\
					&\quad-\lambda \left( P(E_n;\partial C_n)+P(E_n; {\rm int} (C)\setminus C_n)+ m(\delta, \eta)| E_n\cap C|+\eta\right)\Big]\\
					&\leq \liminf_n \Big[ \left(P(E_n; \mathbb R^N\setminus C)+P(E_n; {\rm int} (C)\setminus C_n)\right) -\lambda P(E_n;\partial C_n)\\
					&\quad+ m(\delta, \eta)| E_n\cap C|+\eta\Big]\\
					&=\liminf_n\mathcal F_{C_n}(E_n)+\eta.
				\end{align}
				By the generality of $\eta> 0$, we get \eqref{eq: liminf}.

                Let us now prove the $\limsup$ inequality.
                Notice that, by the general theory of $\Gamma$-convergence, see e.g.\ \cite[Proposition 8.3]{dalmasoIntroductionGConvergence1993}, it is enough to prove it for every subsequence of a given subsequence.
                By \Cref{lemma: aggiustamento convessi}, we may assume, up to some dilations and a subsequence, that $C\subset C_{n+1}\subset C_{n}$ for all $n$. 
                Moreover, by \Cref{rmk: convergenza convessi}, $|\mu_{ C_n}|  \xrightharpoonup{*} |\mu_{ C}|$ in duality with $C^0_c(\mathbb R^N)$. 
                Finally, by \Cref{lm: approssimazione}, we may assume that $E=F\setminus C$, where $F$ is a bounded open set with $C^\infty$ boundary such that $P(F,\partial C)=P(F, \partial C_n)=0$ for all $n$, so, in particular, $|\mu_C|(\partial F)=0$.
                Let $E_n=F\setminus C_n$, then $P(E_n; C_n)=|\mu_{C_n}|(F)$ (see \cite[Theorem 16.3]{Ma12}). By \Cref{rmk: convergenza convessi}, $E_n\to E$ in $L^1$.
                Moreover, since $|\mu_C|(\partial F)=0$, it follows $P(E_n; C_n)=|\mu_{C_n}|(F)\to |\mu_C|(F)=P(E;C)$. Finally, 
				$P(E;\mathbb R^N\setminus C)-P(E_n;\mathbb R^N\setminus C_n)=P(F;C_n\setminus C)$: since $C_n\setminus C\searrow\emptyset$, the difference decreases to 0, yielding \eqref{eq: limsup}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor: gamma limite dimensione piena}
				Let $\Lambda>0$ and $C\subset \mathbb R^N$ be a bounded convex body and $(C_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of bounded convex bodies converging to $C$ in the Hausdorff metric. Then:
				\begin{itemize}
					\item for every set $E\subset\mathbb R^N$ of finite perimeter and every sequence $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ such that $E_n\to E$ in $L^1$ and $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_n$, 
					\begin{equation}
						 \mathcal F^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(E)\leq \liminf_n\mathcal F^{v}_{C_n, \Lambda}(E_n);
					\end{equation}
					\item for every set $E\subset\mathbb R^N$ of finite perimeter and volume there exist a sequence $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ such that $E_n\to E$ strictly, $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_n$ and 
					\begin{equation}
						\mathcal F^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(E)=\lim_n \mathcal F^{v}_{C_n, \Lambda}(E_n).
					\end{equation}
				\end{itemize}
				In other words, $\mathcal F^{v}_{C, \Lambda}=\Gamma \text{-}\lim\limits_{n\to +\infty} \mathcal F^{v}_{C_n, \Lambda}$ in $L^1$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lemma: funzioni calotte} 
				Let $(C_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of convex bodies converging in the sense of Hausdorff to $C$, a bounded convex body in $\{x_N=0\}$. Then, for each $\varepsilon>0$, it holds:
				\begin{itemize}
					\item $f_n^u, f_n^l$ are eventually well defined on the whole of $C_\varepsilon$ and are concave and convex, respectively;
					\item $f_n^u, f_n^l\to 0$ uniformly on $\tilde{C}_n$ (in particular, eventually on $C_\varepsilon$);
					\item $\operatorname{Lip} f_n^u, \operatorname{Lip} f_n^l\to 0$ in $C_\varepsilon$.
				\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Notice that since $C_n\to C$ with respect to Hausdorff distance, $\tilde{C}_n\coloneqq p(C_n)\to C$. In particular $C_\varepsilon \subset \tilde{C}_n$ eventually. Moreover, it is immediate to check that $\sup |f^u_n|, \sup |f^l_n|\leq d_{\mathcal{H}}(C_n, C)\to 0$, thus $\operatorname{osc} f_n^u, \operatorname{osc} f_n^l\leq 2 d_{\mathcal{H}}(C_n, C)\to 0$. The third item follows by well known properties of convex functions.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor: convergence measures}
				Let $C$ be a bounded convex body in $\{x_N=0\}$ and let $(C_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of convex bodies in $\mathbb R^N$ Hausdorff converging to $C$. Set $\tilde{C_n}=p(C_n)$ and $f_n^u, f_n^l:\tilde{C_n}\to \mathbb R$, $C_n^u, C_n^l, C_n^s$ as above. Then
				\begin{itemize}
					\item for the Gauss--Green measures it holds
					\begin{equation}
						\left(\mu_{C_n}\right) \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} C_n^u, \left(\mu_{C_n}\right)\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} C_n^l\xrightharpoonup{*} \pm e_N \mathcal H^{N-1} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} C;
					\end{equation}
					\item for the Hausdorff measures of the caps:
					\begin{equation}
						\mathcal H^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}  C_n^u, \mathcal H^{N-1} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} C_n^l \xrightharpoonup{*} \mathcal H^{N-1}\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} C 
					\end{equation}
					and $\mathcal H^{N-1}(C_n^s)\to 0$.
				\end{itemize}
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Since $\tilde{C_n}\to \tilde{C}$ in Hausdorff distance, by \Cref{rmk: convergenza convessi} it holds that $\mathcal H^{N-2}(\partial \tilde{C}_n)\to \mathcal H^{N-2}(\partial \tilde{C})$, where the boundaries are intended as before in $\{x_N=0\}$. In particular, this implies
				\begin{equation}
					\mathcal{H}^{N-1}(C_n^s)\leq 2\mathcal H^{N-2}(\partial \tilde{C}_n)d_{\mathcal{H}}(C_n, C)\leq 2\mathcal H^{N-2}(\partial \tilde{C})d_{\mathcal{H}}(C_n, C)+o(1),
				\end{equation} which vanishes. The other results follow easily from \Cref{lemma: funzioni calotte}, the definition of the cap functions and coarea formula.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop: gamma limsup perdita dimensione}
				Let $C$ be a bounded convex body in $\{x_N=0\}$ and $E\subset\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$ a set of finite perimeter such that $|E|<+\infty$. Let $(C_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of convex bodies such that $C_n\to C$ in the Hausdorff metric. Then there exists a sequence $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ of sets of finite perimeter such that $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C_n$, $E_n\to E$ strictly and  $\mathcal F_{C_n}(E_n)\to \mathcal{F}_C(E)$. 
				Moreover, if $E\Subset B(x, R)$ for some $x\in \mathbb R^N$, $R>0$, the sets $E_n$ can be chosen to be such that $E_n\Subset B(x, R)$.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Fix $\varepsilon>0$ and set $C_n^\varepsilon\coloneqq \Phi^\varepsilon_n(C_n)$, $E_n^\varepsilon\coloneqq E\setminus C_n^\varepsilon$.
				Set 
				\begin{align}
						\partial C_n^\varepsilon&= \left( C_n^\varepsilon\cap C_\varepsilon\right)\cup \left(\partial C_n^\varepsilon\cap \left(C_\varepsilon\times(0, +\infty)\right)\right) \cup \left(\partial C_n^\varepsilon\setminus \left(C_\varepsilon\times\mathbb R\right)\right)\\
						&\eqqcolon C_{n, f}^\varepsilon\cup C_{n, c}^\varepsilon\cup C_{n, r}^\varepsilon.
				\end{align} 
				Notice that, for $n$ sufficiently large, $C_{n, f}^\varepsilon=C_\varepsilon$.
                
				By \Cref{lm: approssimazione}, considering the sequence of convex bodies given by $\{x_N \leq 0 \}$ and $(C_n^{\varepsilon}\cap \left(C_\varepsilon\times(0, +\infty)\right))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$, we may assume that $E\cap\{x_N>0\}=F\cap\{x_N>0\}$, where $F$ is a bounded open set with $C^\infty$ boundary such that $P(F, \{x_N=0\})=0$ and such that $P(F, C_{n,c}^{\varepsilon})=0 $ for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$.
				

                Let us study $P(E_n^\varepsilon; \partial C_n^\varepsilon)$.
				For what concerns the flat part, by \Cref{teo: trace 1} it holds
				\begin{equation}
					P(E_n^\varepsilon; C^\varepsilon_{n, f})=\int_{C^\varepsilon_{n, f}} \operatorname{Tr}_{\partial C_n^\varepsilon}^+ \mathbbm{1}_{E_n^\varepsilon}\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}= \int_{C_\varepsilon} \operatorname{Tr}_{C}^- \mathbbm{1}_E\,d\mathcal H^{N-1},
				\end{equation} for $n$ large enough, where $C_n^\varepsilon$ is oriented by the external normal and $C_\varepsilon$ by $e_N$.
				
				
				By \Cref{cor: convergence measures} and \eqref{eq: bilipschitz}, $\mathcal{H}^{N-1} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} C^\varepsilon_{n, c}\xrightharpoonup{*} \mathcal{H}^{N-1} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} C_\varepsilon$.  By \cite[Theorem 16.3]{Ma12} and the Portmanteau Lemma,
				\begin{align}
					\lim_n P(E_n^\varepsilon, C^\varepsilon_{n, c})&=\lim_n \mathcal{H}^{N-1} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}  C^\varepsilon_{n, c}(F)=\mathcal{H}^{N-1} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} C_\varepsilon (F)\\
					&=\int_{C_\varepsilon} \operatorname{Tr}_{C}^+ \mathbbm{1}_F\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}=\int_{C_\varepsilon} \operatorname{Tr}_{C}^+ \mathbbm{1}_E\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}.
				\end{align}
				Finally, for the rest of the wet part it is easy to estimate 
				\begin{equation}
					\limsup_n P(E_n^\varepsilon, C^\varepsilon_{n, r})\leq\limsup_n \mathcal H^{N-1}(C^\varepsilon_{n, r})\leq 2 \mathcal{H}^{N-1}(C\setminus C_\varepsilon).
				\end{equation}
				Putting all together, 
				\begin{equation}
					\limsup_n \left|P(E_n^\varepsilon; C_n^\varepsilon)-\int_C \left(\operatorname{Tr}_{C}^+ \mathbbm{1}_E+ \operatorname{Tr}_{C}^- \mathbbm{1}_E\right)\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}\right|=o_\varepsilon(1).
				\end{equation}
				Moreover, by Hausdorff convergence and the properties of $\Phi_n^\varepsilon$, it is immediate to see that $P(E_n^\varepsilon, \mathbb R^N\setminus C_n^{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{n \to +\infty} P(E, \mathbb R^N\setminus C)$. From here, we conclude that there exists a vanishing sequence $\varepsilon_n\to 0$ such that $\left(\Phi_n^{\varepsilon_n}\right)^{-1}(E_n^{\varepsilon_n})$ does the job.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop: Gamma liminf caso perdita di dimensione}
				Let $C$ be a bounded convex body in $\{x_N=0\}$ and $E\subset\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C$ a set of finite perimeter such that $|E|<+\infty$. Let $(C_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of convex bodies such that $C_n\to C$ in the Hausdorff convergence sense and $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of sets of finite perimeter such that $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C_n$ and $E_n\to E$ in $L^1$. Then 
				\begin{equation}
					\mathcal F_C(E)\leq \liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal F_{C_n}(E_n).
				\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Fix $\varepsilon>0$. Set $C_n^{+, \varepsilon}\coloneqq \Phi^\varepsilon_n(C_n)$, $E_n^{+, \varepsilon}\coloneqq \Phi^\varepsilon_n(E_n)$, $C_n^{-, \varepsilon}\coloneqq \Psi^\varepsilon_n(C_n)$, $E_n^{-, \varepsilon}\coloneqq \Psi^\varepsilon_n(E_n)$. In order to simplify some computations, with a slight abuse of notation, we will use the notation
                \begin{equation}
                	\mathcal F_K(E; B):=P(E; B\setminus K)-\lambda P(E; \partial K\cap B)
                \end{equation}
                for the localized functional also for sets $K\subset\mathbb{R}^N$ with $\dim(K)=N$ and Borel sets $B$.
				Notice that, by \eqref{eq: bilipschitz} it follows
				\begin{equation}
					\liminf_{n \to +\infty}\mathcal{F}_{C_n}(E_n; \{(x', x_N):x'\in C_\varepsilon, x_N\geq f_n^u(x')\})=\liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal{F}_{C_n^{-, \varepsilon}}(E_n^{-, \varepsilon}; C_\varepsilon\times [0,+\infty))
				\end{equation}
				and 
				\begin{equation}
					\liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal{F}_{C_n}(E_n; \{(x', x_N):x'\in C_\varepsilon, x_N\leq f_n^l(x')\})= \liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal{F}_{C_n^{+, \varepsilon}}(E_n^{+, \varepsilon}; C_\varepsilon\times(-\infty,0]).
				\end{equation}
				By the fact that
				\begin{align}
					 \mathcal{F}_{C_n}(E_n; C_\varepsilon\times\mathbb R)&= \mathcal{F}_{C_n}(E_n; \{(x', x_N):x'\in C_\varepsilon, x_N\geq f_n^u(x')\})\\
					 &\qquad+\mathcal{F}_{C_n}(E_n; \{(x', x_N):x'\in C_\varepsilon, x_N\leq f_n^l(x')\}), 
				\end{align}
				it follows
				\begin{align}
					\liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal{F}_{C_n}(E_n; C_\varepsilon\times\mathbb R)\geq& \liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal{F}_{C_n^{-, \varepsilon}}(E_n^{-, \varepsilon}; C_\varepsilon\times[0,+\infty))\\
					&\qquad+\liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal{F}_{C_n^{+, \varepsilon}}(E_n^{+, \varepsilon}; C_\varepsilon\times(-\infty,0]).
				\end{align}
				Notice moreover that
				$E_n^{+, \varepsilon}, E_n^{-, \varepsilon}\to E$ in $L^1$.
				
				By computations similar to those in the proof of \Cref{prop: semicontinuity}, it follows that
				\begin{equation}
					P(E; C_\varepsilon\times(0, +\infty))-\lambda\int_{C_\varepsilon} \operatorname{Tr}_{C}^+ \mathbbm{1}_E \, d\mathcal H^{N-1}\leq \liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal{F}_{C_n^{-, \varepsilon}}(E_n^{-, \varepsilon}, C_\varepsilon\times[0, +\infty)).
				\end{equation}
				Repeating these arguments on the other side of $C$ we get 
				\begin{equation}
					P(E; C_\varepsilon\times(-\infty, 0))-\lambda\int_{C_\varepsilon} \operatorname{Tr}_{C}^- \mathbbm{1}_E \, d\mathcal H^{N-1}\leq \liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal{F}_{C_n^{+, \varepsilon}}(E_n^{+, \varepsilon}, C_\varepsilon\times(-\infty, 0])
				\end{equation}
				where $C$ is always oriented by $e_N$. Finally, notice that
				\begin{equation}
					P\left(E; \mathbb R^N\setminus \left(\bar{C_\varepsilon}\times\mathbb R\right)\right)\leq \liminf_{n \to +\infty} P\left(E_n; \mathbb R^N\setminus \left(\bar{C_\varepsilon}\times\mathbb R\right)\right),
				\end{equation}
				and that, by \Cref{cor: convergence measures}, we have
				\begin{equation}
					\limsup_{n \to +\infty} P\left(E_n; C_n\setminus\left( C_\varepsilon\times\mathbb R\right)\right) \leq\limsup_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal H^{N-1}  (\partial C_n\setminus\left( C_\varepsilon\times\mathbb R)\right)=o_\varepsilon(1)
				\end{equation}
				and that, similarly, 
				\begin{equation}
					\int_{C\setminus C_\varepsilon} \left(\operatorname{Tr}_{C}^+ \mathbbm{1}_E+ \operatorname{Tr}_{C}^- \mathbbm{1}_E\right) \, d\mathcal H^{N-1}\leq 2\mathcal{H}^{N-1}(C\setminus C_\varepsilon)=o_\varepsilon(1).
				\end{equation}
				Summing up, we get
				\begin{equation}
					\mathcal{F}_C(E)\leq \liminf_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal F_{C_n}(E_n)+o_{\varepsilon}(1).
				\end{equation}
				By the arbitrariness of $\varepsilon$, the thesis follows.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{teo: gamma conv}
				Let $\Lambda>0$, $C\subset \mathbb R^N$ be a compact convex set, $(C_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of closed convex sets converging to $C$ in the Hausdorff metric and let $v_n \to v >0$ be a sequence of positive numbers. Then:
				\begin{itemize}
					\item for every set $E\subset\mathbb R^N$ of finite perimeter and every sequence $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ such that $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C_n$ and $E_n\to E$ in $L^1$, 
					\begin{equation}
						\mathcal {F}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(E)\leq \liminf_n\mathcal {F}^{v_n}_{C_n, \Lambda}(E_n);
					\end{equation}
					\item for every set $E\subset\mathbb R^N$ of finite perimeter and finite volume there exists a sequence $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ such that $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^N\setminus C_n$ and $E_n\to E$ strictly and 
					\begin{equation}
						\mathcal{F}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(E)=\lim_n \mathcal{F}^{v_n}_{C_n, \Lambda}(E_n).
					\end{equation}
					Moreover, if $E\Subset B(x, R)$ for some $x\in \mathbb R^N$, $R>0$, the sets $E_n$ can be chosen to be such that $E_n\Subset B(x, R)$.
				\end{itemize}
                In other words, $\mathcal{F}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}=\Gamma\text{-}\lim\limits_{n \to +\infty} \mathcal{F}^{v_n}_{C_n, \Lambda}$, where the $\Gamma$-limit holds both with respect to the
                $L^1$ topology and with respect to the strict topology in $BV$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
If $\dim C<N-1$, we have that  $\mathcal H^{N-1}(\partial C_n)\to 0$, so the thesis is immediate.
                Suppose that $\dim (C) \geq N-1$. By semicontinuity of the dimension, then, $\liminf\limits_{n \to +\infty} \dim(C_n)\geq N-1$. 
                Up to partitioning the set of indices, it is enough to prove the statement in the two cases where $\dim(C_n)\equiv N-1$ and $\dim(C_n)\equiv N$. In the case where $\dim(C_n)\equiv N$, the thesis follows from \Cref{cor: gamma limite dimensione piena} and \Cref{prop: gamma limsup perdita dimensione,prop: Gamma liminf caso perdita di dimensione}.

                Let us finally deal with the case where $\dim(C_n)\equiv N-1=\dim(C)$.
                Let us start by proving the $\liminf$-inequality.
                Let $E$ be a set of finite perimeter and $(E_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be a sequence of sets of finite perimeter such that $E_n\to E$ in $L^1$ and $E_n \subset \mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus C_n$.
                Without loss of generality, we can assume that $\sup \mathcal F_{C_n, \Lambda}^v(E_n)<+\infty$.
                
                For $h>0$, let $C_{n, h}=C_n\times[-h, h]$.
                By \Cref{prop: gamma limsup perdita dimensione} and a diagonal argument, there exists a vanishing sequence $(h_n)_{n\in \mathbb N}$ and a sequence $n\mapsto \tilde E_n\subset \mathbb{R}^N \setminus C_{n, h_n}$ such that 
                \begin{equation}\label{eq: approssimazione limsup}
                    |\mathcal F_{C_n, \Lambda}^v(E_n)-\mathcal F_{C_{n, h_n}, \Lambda}^v(\tilde E_n)|\to 0.
                \end{equation}
                Now, since $h_n\to 0$, $C_{n, h_n}\to C$ in the Hausdorff sense.
                In particular, by \Cref{prop: Gamma liminf caso perdita di dimensione} and \eqref{eq: approssimazione limsup}, 
                \begin{equation}
                    \liminf_n \mathcal F_{C_n, \Lambda}^v(E_n)= \liminf_n \mathcal F_{C_{n, h_n}, \Lambda}^v(\tilde E_n)\geq  \mathcal F_{C, \Lambda}^v(E).
                \end{equation}

                Finally, let us prove the $\limsup$-inequality.
                Up to a sequence of isometries converging to the identity, we can assume that $C_n\subset \{x_N=0\}$ for all $n$.
                Moreover, up to a sequence of rescalings as in \Cref{lemma: aggiustamento convessi}, we can assume that $C\subset C_n$ for all $n$.
                Notice that, by the Hausdorff convergence, this implies that $\mathcal H^{N-1}(C_n\setminus C)\to 0$. 
                Let $E$ be a set of finite perimeter and volume.
                Let $E_n=E\setminus C_n$.
                Then
                \begin{align}
                    \mathcal F_{C_n}(E_n)&= P\left(E;\mathbb{R}^N\setminus {C_n}\right)-\lambda \int_{C_n} \left(\operatorname{Tr}_{\{x_N=0\}}^+ \mathbbm{1}_E+ \operatorname{Tr}_{\{x_N=0\}}^- \mathbbm{1}_E\right)\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}\\
                    &\to P\left(E;\mathbb{R}^N\setminus C\right)-\lambda \int_C \left(\operatorname{Tr}_{C}^+ \mathbbm{1}_E+ \operatorname{Tr}_{C}^- \mathbbm{1}_E\right)\,d\mathcal H^{N-1}= \mathcal{F}_{C}(E),
                \end{align}
                concluding the proof.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
Let $C\subset\mathbb R^N$ be a closed convex set.
            We say that $(\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{C})$ is an \emph{asymptotic configuration} for $C$ if there exist $k\in\mathbb{N} \cup \{ 0 \}$, $C_1, \ldots, C_k$ asymptotic cylinders for $C$, $E_0\subset \mathbb R^N\setminus C$, $E_j\subset\mathbb R^N\setminus C_j$, $j=1, \ldots, k$ sets of finite perimeter such that \begin{equation}
				\mathbf E=(E_0, \ldots, E_k), \qquad \mathbf C=(C,C_1, \ldots, C_k).
			\end{equation}
			We say that $(\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{C})$ has length $k$ and volume $|\mathbf E|=\sum_{j=0}^k |E_j|$.
            
            In order to avoid trivial situations, we assume that $|E_j|>0$ for $j>0$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
One might expect the existence of connected components $\Omega$ such that $\@ifnextchar^{{ 
	\setbox0\hbox{${\save@mathaccent"0362{\Omega}}^H$} 
	\setbox2\hbox{${\save@mathaccent"0362{\kern0pt\Omega}}^H$} 
	\ifdim\ht0=\ht2  
	\begingroup
	\def\mathaccent#\Omega#0{ 
		                           
		\let\mathaccent\save@mathaccent
		                                                                                              
		\if22 \let \fi
		                                 
		\setbox\z@\hbox{$\macc@style{}_{}$} 
		\setbox\tw@\hbox{$\macc@style{}{}_{}$} 
		\dimen@\wd\tw@
		\advance\dimen@-\wd\z@
		                                                    
		\divide\dimen@ 3
		\@tempdima\wd\tw@
		\advance\@tempdima-\scriptspace
		                                           
		\divide\@tempdima 10
		\advance\dimen@-\@tempdima
		                                                                            
		\ifdim\dimen@>\z@ \dimen@0pt\fi

            However, this cannot happen, as it can be shown arguing as in \cite[Theorem 3.2, step 5]{FoFuLeMo23} (see also \Cref{cor: le componenti devono toccare il convesso}).
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
Let $\Lambda>0$ and let $(\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{C})$ be an asymptotic configuration. We say that $(\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{C})$ is an \emph{asymptotic} (or  \emph{generalized}) \emph{minimizer} for $\mathcal G^{v}_{C, \Lambda}$ if 
			\begin{equation}
				\mathcal G^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(\mathbf E, \mathbf C)\leq \mathcal G^{v}_{C, \Lambda}( \tilde{\mathbf E}, \tilde{\mathbf C}),
			\end{equation}
			where $( \tilde{\mathbf E}, \tilde{\mathbf C})$ varies among all asymptotic configurations (regardless of the lengths) and $E_i \neq \emptyset$ for every $i=1,\dots, k$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{teo: penal unb}
			For every $\Lambda>0$  there exist an asymptotic minimizer $(\mathbf E, \mathbf C)$ for the functional $\mathcal G^{v}_{C, \Lambda}$.
			Moreover,
			\begin{equation}
				\inf \mathcal F^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(F)=\min \mathcal G^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(\tilde{\mathbf E}; \tilde{\mathbf C})=\mathcal G^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(\mathbf E; \mathbf C),
			\end{equation}
			where $F\subset \mathbb R^N\setminus C$ varies among all sets of finite perimeter and $(\tilde{\mathbf E}; \tilde{\mathbf C})$ varies among all asymptotic configurations.
			Finally, if $\mathbf{E}=(E_0, \ldots, E_k)$, $\mathbf{C}=(C,C_1, \ldots, C_k)$ and $C_j=\lim\limits_{n \to +\infty} \left(C-x_n^{(j)}\right)$, $j=1, \ldots, k$ in the sense of Kuratowski for some diverging sequences $\left(x_n^{(j)}\right)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\subset\partial C$, then there exist a sequence $(\tilde F_n)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ of sets of finite perimeter and $k+1$ sequences $\left(\tilde F_n^{(j)}\right)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ in $\mathbb R^N\setminus C$ with the following properties:
			\begin{itemize}
				\item for all $n\in \mathbb N$, the sets $\tilde F_n^{(i)}$, $\tilde F_n^{(j)}$ have positive distance when $i\neq j$;
				\item for all $n\in \mathbb N$, $\tilde F_n=\bigcup\limits_{j=0}^{k} \tilde F_n^{(j)}$;
				\item $\tilde F^{(j)}_n-x^{(j)}_n\xrightarrow{n \to +\infty} E_j$ strictly;
				\item $\mathcal F_{C}(\tilde F_n^{(j)})\xrightarrow{n \to +\infty} \mathcal F_{C_j}(E_j)$.
			\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
\textbf{Step 1:} We begin by proving that the two infima coincide. First, note that the infimum of $\mathcal{G}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}$ is non--negative and bounded above by $P(B)$, where $B\subset\mathbb R^N$ is some ball of volume $v$.
			
			Let $\varepsilon>0$. Then there exist $k\in\mathbb N$ and an asymptotic configuration $(\mathbf E, \mathbf C)$  of length $k$, where $\mathbf E=(E_0, \ldots, E_k)$, with $E_j$ bounded for $j=0, \ldots, k$,  and $\mathbf C=(C,C_1, \ldots, C_k)$, such that
			\begin{equation}
				\mathcal{G}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(\mathbf E; \mathbf C)\leq \inf \mathcal{G}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(\tilde{\mathbf E}; \tilde{\mathbf C})+\varepsilon,
			\end{equation}
			where $(\tilde{\mathbf E}, \tilde{\mathbf C})$ varies among all possible asymptotic configurations.
			Let us now exploit the approximating sequences granted by \Cref{teo: gamma conv} in order to approximate the sets $E_j$ with sequences in $\mathbb R^N\setminus C$.
			
			Let $1\leq j\leq k$ be fixed. Let $R_j>0$ be such that $E_j\Subset B(0, R_j)$. 
			Now, by definition of asymptotic cylinder and \Cref{lemma: local hausdorff} (notice that $0\in C_j$ by definition), there exists $\left(x^{(j)}_n\right)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ such that $\left(C-x_n^{(j)}\right)\cap \@ifnextchar^{{

			Let us now choose our sets $\tilde F_n^{(j)}$. For $j=0$, we set $\tilde F_n^{(0)}=E_0$ for all $n\in \mathbb N$.
			Let now $j=1$. Since $x_n^{(1)}$ diverges, we can set $m_1^{(1)}$ as the first $n$ such that $A_n^{(1)}+x^{(1)}_n$ has positive distance from $E_0$. Let $2 \leq j \leq k$ and suppose to have defined $m_1^{(l)}$ for $l < j$. We define $m_1^{(j)}$ to be the first natural number $n$ such that $A_n^{(j)}+x^{(j)}_n$ has positive distance from $E_0, A_{m_1^{(l)}}^{(l)}+x^{(l)}_{m_1^{(l)}}$ for $l=1, \ldots, j-1$.
			We can then pass to $m_2^{(1)}$, by choosing it as the first $n$ such that $A_n^{(1)}+x^{(1)}_n$ has positive distance from all the previously selected sets.
			Proceeding recursively, we obtain k sequences $\left(m_n^{(j)} \right)_{n\in \mathbb N, j=1, \ldots, k}$ such that, setting
			\begin{equation}
				\tilde F_n^{(j)}=A_{m_n^{(j)}}+x^{(j)}_{m_n^{(j)}},
			\end{equation}
			and relabelling $x^{(j)}_{m_n^{(j)}}$ as $x_n^{(j)}$, we have that:
			\begin{itemize}
				\item $d(\tilde F_n^{(i)}, \tilde F_n^{(j)})>0$ when $i\neq j$;
				\item $E_j=\lim\limits_{n \to +\infty} \tilde F_n^{(j)}-x_n^{(j)}$ strictly;
				\item $\mathcal F_{C}(\tilde F_n^{(j)})\to \mathcal F_{C}(E_j)$.
			\end{itemize}
			Moreover, since the sets are at positive distance from each other, if we set $\tilde F_n=\bigcup\limits_{j=0}^k \tilde F_n^{(j)}$, we get that
			\begin{equation}
				\mathcal{F}_C(\tilde F_n)=\sum_{j=0}^k \mathcal{F}_C(\tilde F_n^{(j)})= \mathcal{F}_C(E_0)+\sum_{j=1}^k \mathcal F_{(C-x_n^{(j)})\cap B(0, R_j)}(A_n^{(j)}).
			\end{equation}
			By definition of the $A_n^{(j)}$, we have that 
			\begin{equation}
				\inf \mathcal{F}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(F)\leq \lim_n \mathcal{F}_C(\tilde F_n)= \mathcal{G}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(\mathbf E;\mathbf C)\leq \inf \mathcal{G}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(\tilde{\mathbf E}; \tilde{\mathbf C})+\varepsilon,
			\end{equation}
			where $F\subset\mathbb R^N\setminus C$ varies among all sets of finite perimeter and $(\tilde{\mathbf E}, \tilde{\mathbf C})$ varies among all possible asymptotic configurations for $C$.
			Since $\varepsilon>0$ is arbitrary, it follows that $\inf \mathcal{F}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(F)\leq\inf \mathcal{G}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(\tilde{\mathbf E}; \tilde{\mathbf C})$. The other inequality is trivial, since all possible competitor sets for $\mathcal {F}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}$ yield an asymptotic configuration for $C$.
			
			\noindent\textbf{Step 2:} we prove that the infimum for $\mathcal{G}^{v}_{C, \Lambda}$ is indeed a minimum. Let $F_n^{(1)}, \ldots, F_n^{(M)}$ for $n\in \mathbb N$ and $(x_n^{(j)})_{n\in \mathbb N}$ be the sequences chosen at the beginning of \Cref{section: existence}.
			The behaviour of the sets $F_n^{(j)}$ for $j>M_1$ is easier to describe: up to subsequences, their union converge in $L^1$ to a set $E_0\subset\mathbb R^N\setminus C$ such that, by \Cref{prop: semicontinuity}, satisfies
			\begin{equation}
				\mathcal F^{v}_{C, \Lambda}(E_0)\leq \liminf_{n\to +\infty}\mathcal {F}^{v}_{C, \Lambda} \left( \bigcup_{j=M_1+1}^{M} F_n^{(j)}\right).
			\end{equation}
			
			The components $F^{(j)}_n$ with $j\leq M_1$ are treated differently. Up to extracting a subsequence, we may assume that $C-x_n^{(j)}\to C_j$ in the sense of Kuratowski and that $F_n^{(j)}-x_n^{(j)} \to E_j$ in $L^1$, where $E_j\subset\mathbb R^N\setminus C_j$, for $n\to \infty$ and $j=1, \ldots, M_1$.
			Now, since $ \left(C-x_n^{(j)}\right)\cap \@ifnextchar^{{
